People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, uh… I’m here because this thing at work has just been going on too long. Over a year now, really. They changed the ticket rotation, and then these security audits started getting more pointed, and it’s… I know how it looks when I say it, but it feels like they’re building a paper trail against me.

I work in computer support, so I know logs, I know audits, I know how policies get used. Management keeps showing me reports and saying, “See, it’s just policy, everyone’s in the same system,” but that’s the thing—systems can still be used a certain way. I’ve seen it before, not exactly like this, but years ago we had this manager who would always say everything was “process,” and then suddenly somebody was out the door.

So I guess I’m here because no one at work will take it seriously, and I need someone to understand that this isn’t just me being difficult.

Mostly me, yeah. I mean, I can’t say nobody else ever gets audited or has tickets reassigned—of course they do, it’s computer support, there are always queues and reports and all that. And maybe that’s what bothers me, because years ago at another place they did this sort of thing where everything was “just procedure,” and then later you find out they were documenting someone the whole time. So when I see the same pattern for over a year, I don’t think it’s random. I think they’re using the system to build a paper trail against me.

I’ve seen it before, not exactly like this, but years ago we had this manager who would always say everything was “process,” and then suddenly somebody was out the door.\n\nSo I guess I’m here because no one at work will take it seriously, and I need someone to understand that this isn’t just me being difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your coworkers or management are intentionally trying to harm you or set you up to be fired?\n\nPatient: I mean… “harm” sounds a little dramatic, like they’re trying to hurt me personally, but set me up? Yeah, that’s what it feels like. For over a year now, the ticket rotation changes and these security audits have been lining up in a way that makes me look bad, and then management keeps pulling out logs like, “No, see, it’s just policy.”\n\nBut I work with systems. I know how logs can be used. It’s not that the computer is lying—it’s who decides what to look at and when. And I’ve seen workplaces do this kind of thing before, where everything is framed as normal procedure until suddenly there’s enough paperwork to justify getting rid of someone. So yes, I think they’re building a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that people at work are specifically targeting you, or is this something you’ve noticed happening to others as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly me, yeah. I mean, I can’t say nobody else ever gets audited or has tickets reassigned—of course they do, it’s computer support, there are always queues and reports and all that. But the way it lines up with me… the new ticket rotation, then these security audits getting more pointed, and then management showing me logs like that proves it’s all neutral—it feels directed.\n\nAnd maybe that’s what bothers me, because years ago at another place they did this sort of thing where everything was “just procedure,” and then later you find out they were documenting someone the whole time. So when I see the same pattern for over a year, I don’t think it’s random. I think they’re using the system to build a paper trail against me.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed anyone outside of work, like neighbors or people in public, acting in a way that makes you feel like they’re also targeting or watching you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 60 / computer_support_specialist / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work, even though management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

No, not really outside of work. I mean, people act strange in public sometimes, but I don’t think my neighbors are part of it or anything like that.

And I’ve seen workplaces do this kind of thing before, where everything is framed as normal procedure until suddenly there’s enough paperwork to justify getting rid of someone. So yes, I think they’re building a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that people at work are specifically targeting you, or is this something you’ve noticed happening to others as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly me, yeah. I mean, I can’t say nobody else ever gets audited or has tickets reassigned—of course they do, it’s computer support, there are always queues and reports and all that. But the way it lines up with me… the new ticket rotation, then these security audits getting more pointed, and then management showing me logs like that proves it’s all neutral—it feels directed.\n\nAnd maybe that’s what bothers me, because years ago at another place they did this sort of thing where everything was “just procedure,” and then later you find out they were documenting someone the whole time. So when I see the same pattern for over a year, I don’t think it’s random. I think they’re using the system to build a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed anyone outside of work, like neighbors or people in public, acting in a way that makes you feel like they’re also targeting or watching you?\n\nPatient: No, not really outside of work. I mean, people act strange in public sometimes, but I don’t think my neighbors are part of it or anything like that.\n\nIt’s really the work stuff. The ticket rotation changing, then the security audits getting more pointed, and management showing me logs like, “See, it’s just policy.” That’s what I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously for over a year. It’s not random to me—it feels like they’re using all that to build a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like random events or unrelated actions by strangers are directed at you or have a special meaning for you personally?\n\nPatient: Not random strangers, no. I’m not walking down the street thinking some guy at the grocery store is sending me a message or anything like that.\n\nWhat I’m here about is work. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Outside of work? No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had bad luck with people in offices before—years ago there was a manager who would smile in meetings and then suddenly you’d get these little notes in your file, and everybody pretended it was normal procedure. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits at work are being used to build a paper trail against me. I think they’re using the system to build a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed anyone outside of work, like neighbors or people in public, acting in a way that makes you feel like they’re also targeting or watching you?\n\nPatient: No, not really outside of work. It’s not random to me—it feels like they’re using all that to build a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like random events or unrelated actions by strangers are directed at you or have a special meaning for you personally?\n\nPatient: Not random strangers, no. I’m not walking down the street thinking some guy at the grocery store is sending me a message or anything like that.\n\nWhat I’m here about is work. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. So no, not strangers—this is the workplace stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts that people were plotting against you or that events were directed at you outside of this work situation?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? No, I don’t think so. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean, “organized” makes it sound like some movie thing, like people in a dark room with charts on the wall, and I’m not saying that. But deliberate? Yes, I think so.

The ticket rotation changed, then the security audits started landing in this very particular way, and over time it looks like they’re building a paper trail against me. I’ve worked in computer support long enough to know logs don’t just speak for themselves. Somebody decides what gets tracked, what gets pulled up, what gets treated like a problem.

Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” but that’s exactly the point. Policy can be used a certain way. And maybe other people don’t see it because they’re not the one it’s aimed at, or they don’t know the systems like I do.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Secret plan… I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly. That sounds a little dramatic. But I do think the ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build something against me, yes.

I mean, I’ve been in computer support a long time, and systems like that don’t just magically decide what matters. Somebody sets them up, somebody decides which logs to show, which incidents get highlighted. Management keeps saying, “It’s just policy,” and showing me reports, but that doesn’t reassure me. Policies can be used to make a paper trail look objective.

So, shared plan? Maybe not like they all sat down and wrote it out. But I do believe people at work are targeting me specifically through those changes.

Management keeps pulling out logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but I know how those systems work—what gets logged, what gets highlighted, when it gets brought up. That’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the way the ticket rotation and security audits are being handled at work is part of a deliberate, organized effort against you, even if others don’t see it the same way?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, “organized” makes it sound like some movie thing, like people in a dark room with charts on the wall, and I’m not saying that. But deliberate? Yes, I think so.\n\nThe ticket rotation changed, then the security audits started landing in this very particular way, and over time it looks like they’re building a paper trail against me. I’ve worked in computer support long enough to know logs don’t just speak for themselves. Somebody decides what gets tracked, what gets pulled up, what gets treated like a problem.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” but that’s exactly the point. Policy can be used a certain way. And maybe other people don’t see it because they’re not the one it’s aimed at, or they don’t know the systems like I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people involved in changing the ticket rotation and conducting the security audits at work are acting on a shared, secret plan to target you specifically?\n\nPatient: Secret plan… I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly. That sounds a little dramatic. But I do think the ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build something against me, yes.\n\nI mean, I’ve been in computer support a long time, and systems like that don’t just magically decide what matters. Somebody sets them up, somebody decides which logs to show, which incidents get highlighted. Management keeps saying, “It’s just policy,” and showing me reports, but that doesn’t reassure me. Policies can be used to make a paper trail look objective.\n\nSo, shared plan? Maybe not like they all sat down and wrote it out. But I do believe people at work are targeting me specifically through those changes.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel that events, objects, or people’s actions in general — even unrelated to work — have a special, personal meaning for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not in the general sense. I mean, I know what you’re asking—like whether I see messages in random things or think strangers are signaling to me. That’s not really it.

This is about work. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. And every time I bring it up, management shows me logs and says, “It’s just policy,” like that settles it.

But I’ve worked around systems long enough to know logs depend on what people choose to track and how they choose to present it. Sorry, I know I keep coming back to that, but that’s the part people don’t seem to hear. It’s not everything having some hidden meaning. It’s this specific situation.

Management keeps showing me reports and saying, “See, it’s just policy, everyone’s in the same system,” but that’s the thing—systems can still be used a certain way. I’ve seen it before, not exactly like this, but years ago we had this manager who would always say everything was “process,” and then suddenly somebody was out the door.\n\nSo I guess I’m here because no one at work will take it seriously, and I need someone to understand that this isn’t just me being difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your coworkers or management are intentionally trying to harm you or set you up to be fired?\n\nPatient: I mean… “harm” sounds a little dramatic, like they’re trying to hurt me personally, but set me up? Yeah, that’s what it feels like. For over a year now, the ticket rotation changes and these security audits have been lining up in a way that makes me look bad, and then management keeps pulling out logs like, “No, see, it’s just policy.”\n\nBut I work with systems. I know how logs can be used. It’s not that the computer is lying—it’s who decides what to look at and when. And I’ve seen workplaces do this kind of thing before, where everything is framed as normal procedure until suddenly there’s enough paperwork to justify getting rid of someone. So yes, I think they’re building a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that people at work are specifically targeting you, or is this something you’ve noticed happening to others as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly me, yeah. I mean, I can’t say nobody else ever gets audited or has tickets reassigned—of course they do, it’s computer support, there are always queues and reports and all that. But the way it lines up with me… the new ticket rotation, then these security audits getting more pointed, and then management showing me logs like that proves it’s all neutral—it feels directed.\n\nAnd maybe that’s what bothers me, because years ago at another place they did this sort of thing where everything was “just procedure,” and then later you find out they were documenting someone the whole time. So when I see the same pattern for over a year, I don’t think it’s random. I think they’re using the system to build a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed anyone outside of work, like neighbors or people in public, acting in a way that makes you feel like they’re also targeting or watching you?\n\nPatient: No, not really outside of work. I mean, people act strange in public sometimes, but I don’t think my neighbors are part of it or anything like that.\n\nIt’s really the work stuff. The ticket rotation changing, then the security audits getting more pointed, and management showing me logs like, “See, it’s just policy.” That’s what I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously for over a year. It’s not random to me—it feels like they’re using all that to build a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like random events or unrelated actions by strangers are directed at you or have a special meaning for you personally?\n\nPatient: Not random strangers, no. I’m not walking down the street thinking some guy at the grocery store is sending me a message or anything like that.\n\nWhat I’m here about is work. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. And management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” but I’ve worked with systems long enough to know it depends on what they choose to track and when they choose to bring it up. So no, not strangers—this is the workplace stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts that people were plotting against you or that events were directed at you outside of this work situation?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? No, I don’t think so. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits at work are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t mean it’s neutral.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people were trying to harm you or that events were directed at you in situations outside of work, such as in your personal life or other settings?\n\nPatient: In my personal life? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s basically why I’m here, I guess.

I’ve tried to explain it at work, and people either don’t understand or they act like I’m making connections that aren’t there. But for over a year now, I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me.

And, you know, I worked in computer support long enough to know how these systems look from the inside. It reminds me of when we changed a helpdesk system years ago and suddenly the numbers made one whole team look worse, just because of how categories were assigned. People forget that. They look at a report and think it’s neutral.

Management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s “just policy,” but that’s exactly the sort of thing that can make it harder to explain. Because on paper it looks routine. But I don’t think it is. So yes, I have an idea that other people don’t seem to understand, even when I lay it out pretty clearly.

But the way it lines up with me… the new ticket rotation, then these security audits getting more pointed, and then management showing me logs like that proves it’s all neutral—it feels directed.\n\nAnd maybe that’s what bothers me, because years ago at another place they did this sort of thing where everything was “just procedure,” and then later you find out they were documenting someone the whole time. So when I see the same pattern for over a year, I don’t think it’s random. I think they’re using the system to build a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed anyone outside of work, like neighbors or people in public, acting in a way that makes you feel like they’re also targeting or watching you?\n\nPatient: No, not really outside of work. I mean, people act strange in public sometimes, but I don’t think my neighbors are part of it or anything like that.\n\nIt’s really the work stuff. The ticket rotation changing, then the security audits getting more pointed, and management showing me logs like, “See, it’s just policy.” That’s what I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously for over a year. It’s not random to me—it feels like they’re using all that to build a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like random events or unrelated actions by strangers are directed at you or have a special meaning for you personally?\n\nPatient: Not random strangers, no. I’m not walking down the street thinking some guy at the grocery store is sending me a message or anything like that.\n\nWhat I’m here about is work. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. And management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” but I’ve worked with systems long enough to know it depends on what they choose to track and when they choose to bring it up. So no, not strangers—this is the workplace stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts that people were plotting against you or that events were directed at you outside of this work situation?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had bad luck with people in offices before—years ago there was a manager who would smile in meetings and then suddenly you’d get these little notes in your file, and everybody pretended it was normal procedure. That’s the kind of thing that makes you pay attention.\n\nBut no, I’m not saying the world in general is plotting against me. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits at work are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t mean it’s neutral.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people were trying to harm you or that events were directed at you in situations outside of work, such as in your personal life or other settings?\n\nPatient: In my personal life? No, not really. I mean, I’ve had arguments with people like anyone else, neighbors being noisy, that kind of thing, but I don’t think they’re trying to harm me.\n\nThe thing I keep coming back to is work. That’s where it’s been happening. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to see that the ticket rotation changed, and then the security audits got more pointed, and it’s like they’re building a paper trail on me. Management keeps pulling out logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but I know how those systems work—what gets logged, what gets highlighted, when it gets brought up. That’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the way the ticket rotation and security audits are being handled at work is part of a deliberate, organized effort against you, even if others don’t see it the same way?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, “organized” makes it sound like some movie thing, like people in a dark room with charts on the wall, and I’m not saying that. But deliberate? Yes, I think so.\n\nThe ticket rotation changed, then the security audits started landing in this very particular way, and over time it looks like they’re building a paper trail against me. I’ve worked in computer support long enough to know logs don’t just speak for themselves. Somebody decides what gets tracked, what gets pulled up, what gets treated like a problem.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” but that’s exactly the point. Policy can be used a certain way. And maybe other people don’t see it because they’re not the one it’s aimed at, or they don’t know the systems like I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people involved in changing the ticket rotation and conducting the security audits at work are acting on a shared, secret plan to target you specifically?\n\nPatient: Secret plan… I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly. But I do think the ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build something against me, yes.\n\nI mean, I’ve been in computer support a long time, and systems like that don’t just magically decide what matters. Somebody sets them up, somebody decides which logs to show, which incidents get highlighted. Management keeps saying, “It’s just policy,” and showing me reports, but that doesn’t reassure me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Outside of work? I don’t think so, not in any big way.

I mean, everybody has things they’re sure about when other people don’t agree. Like my brother and I argued for years about whether my father should’ve kept old receipts for tax records—he used to have boxes of them in the basement, stacked by year, and it drove my mother crazy. So I know people can disagree and each person thinks they’re right.

But if you mean something like this, where I keep trying to explain it and people say I’m mistaken… no, I can’t really think of another example outside work. This is the one that’s been hard to get across.

I mean… yes, I know people think it sounds strange. “Impossible,” maybe, depending on who you ask. Management definitely acts like it’s impossible.

But I can’t really see it any other way. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits aren’t just routine. They’re being used to build a paper trail against me at work. And every time I say that, they pull out logs and say, “See, it’s just policy,” but that doesn’t convince me.

It’s like—sorry, this reminds me of when we used to troubleshoot printer queues, where the error message wasn’t the real problem, it was just what the system decided to show you. People would stare at the print log like it was the whole truth, but if you knew where to look, you could tell something else was happening underneath.

So yes, I know other people think I’m reading too much into it. But from where I’m sitting, it fits too well to ignore.

They changed the ticket rotation, and then these security audits started getting more pointed, and it’s… I know how it looks when I say it, but it feels like they’re building a paper trail against me.\n\nI work in computer support, so I know logs, I know audits, I know how policies get used. Management keeps showing me reports and saying, “See, it’s just policy, everyone’s in the same system,” but that’s the thing—systems can still be used a certain way. I’ve seen it before, not exactly like this, but years ago we had this manager who would always say everything was “process,” and then suddenly somebody was out the door.\n\nSo I guess I’m here because no one at work will take it seriously, and I need someone to understand that this isn’t just me being difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your coworkers or management are intentionally trying to harm you or set you up to be fired?\n\nPatient: I mean… “harm” sounds a little dramatic, like they’re trying to hurt me personally, but set me up? Yeah, that’s what it feels like. For over a year now, the ticket rotation changes and these security audits have been lining up in a way that makes me look bad, and then management keeps pulling out logs like, “No, see, it’s just policy.”\n\nBut I work with systems. I know how logs can be used. It’s not that the computer is lying—it’s who decides what to look at and when. And I’ve seen workplaces do this kind of thing before, where everything is framed as normal procedure until suddenly there’s enough paperwork to justify getting rid of someone. So yes, I think they’re building a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that people at work are specifically targeting you, or is this something you’ve noticed happening to others as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly me, yeah. I mean, I can’t say nobody else ever gets audited or has tickets reassigned—of course they do, it’s computer support, there are always queues and reports and all that. But the way it lines up with me… the new ticket rotation, then these security audits getting more pointed, and then management showing me logs like that proves it’s all neutral—it feels directed.\n\nAnd maybe that’s what bothers me, because years ago at another place they did this sort of thing where everything was “just procedure,” and then later you find out they were documenting someone the whole time. So when I see the same pattern for over a year, I don’t think it’s random. I think they’re using the system to build a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed anyone outside of work, like neighbors or people in public, acting in a way that makes you feel like they’re also targeting or watching you?\n\nPatient: No, not really outside of work. I mean, people act strange in public sometimes, but I don’t think my neighbors are part of it or anything like that.\n\nIt’s really the work stuff. The ticket rotation changing, then the security audits getting more pointed, and management showing me logs like, “See, it’s just policy.” That’s what I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously for over a year. It’s not random to me—it feels like they’re using all that to build a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like random events or unrelated actions by strangers are directed at you or have a special meaning for you personally?\n\nPatient: Not random strangers, no. I’m not walking down the street thinking some guy at the grocery store is sending me a message or anything like that.\n\nWhat I’m here about is work. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. And management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” but I’ve worked with systems long enough to know it depends on what they choose to track and when they choose to bring it up. So no, not strangers—this is the workplace stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts that people were plotting against you or that events were directed at you outside of this work situation?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had bad luck with people in offices before—years ago there was a manager who would smile in meetings and then suddenly you’d get these little notes in your file, and everybody pretended it was normal procedure. That’s the kind of thing that makes you pay attention.\n\nBut no, I’m not saying the world in general is plotting against me. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits at work are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t mean it’s neutral.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people were trying to harm you or that events were directed at you in situations outside of work, such as in your personal life or other settings?\n\nPatient: In my personal life? No, not really. I mean, I’ve had arguments with people like anyone else, neighbors being noisy, that kind of thing, but I don’t think they’re trying to harm me.\n\nThe thing I keep coming back to is work. That’s where it’s been happening. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to see that the ticket rotation changed, and then the security audits got more pointed, and it’s like they’re building a paper trail on me. Management keeps pulling out logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but I know how those systems work—what gets logged, what gets highlighted, when it gets brought up. That’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the way the ticket rotation and security audits are being handled at work is part of a deliberate, organized effort against you, even if others don’t see it the same way?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, “organized” makes it sound like some movie thing, like people in a dark room with charts on the wall, and I’m not saying that. But deliberate? Yes, I think so.\n\nThe ticket rotation changed, then the security audits started landing in this very particular way, and over time it looks like they’re building a paper trail against me. I’ve worked in computer support long enough to know logs don’t just speak for themselves. Somebody decides what gets tracked, what gets pulled up, what gets treated like a problem.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” but that’s exactly the point. Policy can be used a certain way. And maybe other people don’t see it because they’re not the one it’s aimed at, or they don’t know the systems like I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people involved in changing the ticket rotation and conducting the security audits at work are acting on a shared, secret plan to target you specifically?\n\nPatient: Secret plan… I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly. That sounds a little dramatic. But I do think the ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build something against me, yes.\n\nI mean, I’ve been in computer support a long time, and systems like that don’t just magically decide what matters. Somebody sets them up, somebody decides which logs to show, which incidents get highlighted. Management keeps saying, “It’s just policy,” and showing me reports, but that doesn’t reassure me. Policies can be used to make a paper trail look objective.\n\nSo, shared plan? Maybe not like they all sat down and wrote it out. But I do believe people at work are targeting me specifically through those changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that events, objects, or people’s actions in general — even unrelated to work — have a special, personal meaning for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Not really in general, no. I don’t walk down the street thinking signs or strangers are about me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s work. That’s the part I keep trying to get across. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. And management keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but I know enough about those systems to know policy can be applied in a certain way. So it’s not like everything has a special meaning to me. It’s this specific thing at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that events, objects, or people’s actions in general — even unrelated to work — have a special, personal meaning for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: No, not in the general sense. I mean, I know what you’re asking—like whether I see messages in random things or think strangers are signaling to me. That’s not really it.\n\nThis is about work. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. And every time I bring it up, management shows me logs and says, “It’s just policy,” like that settles it.\n\nBut I’ve worked around systems long enough to know logs depend on what people choose to track and how they choose to present it. Sorry, I know I keep coming back to that, but that’s the part people don’t seem to hear. It’s not everything having some hidden meaning. It’s this specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs or ideas that other people just can’t seem to understand, even when you’ve tried to explain them clearly?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s basically why I’m here, I guess.\n\nI’ve tried to explain it at work, and people either don’t understand or they act like I’m making connections that aren’t there. But for over a year now, I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me.\n\nAnd, you know, I worked in computer support long enough to know how these systems look from the inside. It reminds me of when we changed a helpdesk system years ago and suddenly the numbers made one whole team look worse, just because of how categories were assigned. People forget that. They look at a report and think it’s neutral.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying it’s “just policy,” but that’s exactly the sort of thing that can make it harder to explain. Because on paper it looks routine. But I don’t think it is. So yes, I have an idea that other people don’t seem to understand, even when I lay it out pretty clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had experiences where you strongly believed something was true, even when others could not see it or thought you were mistaken, outside of this situation at work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I don’t think so, not in any big way.\n\nI mean, everybody has things they’re sure about when other people don’t agree. Like my brother and I argued for years about whether my father should’ve kept old receipts for tax records—he used to have boxes of them in the basement, stacked by year, and it drove my mother crazy. So I know people can disagree and each person thinks they’re right.\n\nBut if you mean something like this, where I keep trying to explain it and people say I’m mistaken… no, I can’t really think of another example outside work. This is the one that’s been hard to get across.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have beliefs or ideas that you know other people think are strange or impossible, even when you can’t see things any other way?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I know people think it sounds strange. “Impossible,” maybe, depending on who you ask. Management definitely acts like it’s impossible.\n\nBut I can’t really see it any other way. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits aren’t just routine. They’re being used to build a paper trail against me at work. And every time I say that, they pull out logs and say, “See, it’s just policy,” but that doesn’t convince me.\n\nIt’s like—sorry, this reminds me of when we used to troubleshoot printer queues, where the error message wasn’t the real problem, it was just what the system decided to show you. People would stare at the print log like it was the whole truth, but if you knew where to look, you could tell something else was happening underneath.\n\nSo yes, I know other people think I’m reading too much into it. But from where I’m sitting, it fits too well to ignore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

This is the one that’s been hard to get across.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have beliefs or ideas that you know other people think are strange or impossible, even when you can’t see things any other way?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I know people think it sounds strange. “Impossible,” maybe, depending on who you ask. They’re being used to build a paper trail against me at work. And every time I say that, they pull out logs and say, “See, it’s just policy,” but that doesn’t convince me.\n\nIt’s like—sorry, this reminds me of when we used to troubleshoot printer queues, where the error message wasn’t the real problem, it was just what the system decided to show you. People would stare at the print log like it was the whole truth, but if you knew where to look, you could tell something else was happening underneath.\n\nSo yes, I know other people think I’m reading too much into it. But from where I’m sitting, it fits too well to ignore.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever believe something is happening or being done to you that other people would say is impossible, even when you can’t prove it or show it to them?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that’s what I’m talking about.

I wouldn’t say “impossible,” exactly, because these systems can be used that way. People act like because there’s a log, or because the audit has a policy name attached to it, that proves nobody is steering it. But for over a year now, I’ve felt—more than felt, really—that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.

And I know I can’t hand you one document that says, “Here, this is the plan.” That’s the problem. It’s like when an old server starts failing intermittently, and everyone says it passed diagnostics, but you know from the pattern that something’s wrong. I used to see that all the time in support, where the official report missed the real issue.

Management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t settle it for me. To me, the way it lines up is too specific.

For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to see that the ticket rotation changed, and then the security audits got more pointed, and it’s like they’re building a paper trail on me. Management keeps pulling out logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but I know how those systems work—what gets logged, what gets highlighted, when it gets brought up. That’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the way the ticket rotation and security audits are being handled at work is part of a deliberate, organized effort against you, even if others don’t see it the same way?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, “organized” makes it sound like some movie thing, like people in a dark room with charts on the wall, and I’m not saying that. But deliberate? Yes, I think so.\n\nThe ticket rotation changed, then the security audits started landing in this very particular way, and over time it looks like they’re building a paper trail against me. I’ve worked in computer support long enough to know logs don’t just speak for themselves. Somebody decides what gets tracked, what gets pulled up, what gets treated like a problem.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” but that’s exactly the point. Policy can be used a certain way. And maybe other people don’t see it because they’re not the one it’s aimed at, or they don’t know the systems like I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people involved in changing the ticket rotation and conducting the security audits at work are acting on a shared, secret plan to target you specifically?\n\nPatient: Secret plan… I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly. That sounds a little dramatic. But I do think the ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build something against me, yes.\n\nI mean, I’ve been in computer support a long time, and systems like that don’t just magically decide what matters. Somebody sets them up, somebody decides which logs to show, which incidents get highlighted. Management keeps saying, “It’s just policy,” and showing me reports, but that doesn’t reassure me. Policies can be used to make a paper trail look objective.\n\nSo, shared plan? Maybe not like they all sat down and wrote it out. But I do believe people at work are targeting me specifically through those changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that events, objects, or people’s actions in general — even unrelated to work — have a special, personal meaning for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Not really in general, no. I don’t walk down the street thinking signs or strangers are about me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s work. That’s the part I keep trying to get across. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. And management keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but I know enough about those systems to know policy can be applied in a certain way. So it’s not like everything has a special meaning to me. It’s this specific thing at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that events, objects, or people’s actions in general — even unrelated to work — have a special, personal meaning for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: No, not in the general sense. I mean, I know what you’re asking—like whether I see messages in random things or think strangers are signaling to me. That’s not really it.\n\nThis is about work. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. And every time I bring it up, management shows me logs and says, “It’s just policy,” like that settles it.\n\nBut I’ve worked around systems long enough to know logs depend on what people choose to track and how they choose to present it. Sorry, I know I keep coming back to that, but that’s the part people don’t seem to hear. It’s not everything having some hidden meaning. It’s this specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs or ideas that other people just can’t seem to understand, even when you’ve tried to explain them clearly?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s basically why I’m here, I guess.\n\nI’ve tried to explain it at work, and people either don’t understand or they act like I’m making connections that aren’t there. But for over a year now, I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me.\n\nAnd, you know, I worked in computer support long enough to know how these systems look from the inside. It reminds me of when we changed a helpdesk system years ago and suddenly the numbers made one whole team look worse, just because of how categories were assigned. People forget that. They look at a report and think it’s neutral.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying it’s “just policy,” but that’s exactly the sort of thing that can make it harder to explain. Because on paper it looks routine. But I don’t think it is. So yes, I have an idea that other people don’t seem to understand, even when I lay it out pretty clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had experiences where you strongly believed something was true, even when others could not see it or thought you were mistaken, outside of this situation at work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I don’t think so, not in any big way.\n\nI mean, everybody has things they’re sure about when other people don’t agree. Like my brother and I argued for years about whether my father should’ve kept old receipts for tax records—he used to have boxes of them in the basement, stacked by year, and it drove my mother crazy. They’re being used to build a paper trail against me at work. People would stare at the print log like it was the whole truth, but if you knew where to look, you could tell something else was happening underneath.\n\nSo yes, I know other people think I’m reading too much into it. But from where I’m sitting, it fits too well to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe something is happening or being done to you that other people would say is impossible, even when you can’t prove it or show it to them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s what I’m talking about.\n\nI wouldn’t say “impossible,” exactly, because these systems can be used that way. People act like because there’s a log, or because the audit has a policy name attached to it, that proves nobody is steering it. But for over a year now, I’ve felt—more than felt, really—that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.\n\nAnd I know I can’t hand you one document that says, “Here, this is the plan.” That’s the problem. Yes, that’s… that’s basically why I’m here. I know most people hear it and think, “No, that couldn’t be happening,” because management keeps showing me logs and saying the ticket rotation is just policy, the security audits are routine.

Somebody sets them up, somebody decides which logs to show, which incidents get highlighted. Management keeps saying, “It’s just policy,” and showing me reports, but that doesn’t reassure me. Policies can be used to make a paper trail look objective.\n\nSo, shared plan? Maybe not like they all sat down and wrote it out. But I do believe people at work are targeting me specifically through those changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that events, objects, or people’s actions in general — even unrelated to work — have a special, personal meaning for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Not really in general, no. I don’t walk down the street thinking signs or strangers are about me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s work. That’s the part I keep trying to get across. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. And management keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but I know enough about those systems to know policy can be applied in a certain way. So it’s not like everything has a special meaning to me. It’s this specific thing at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that events, objects, or people’s actions in general — even unrelated to work — have a special, personal meaning for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: No, not in the general sense. I mean, I know what you’re asking—like whether I see messages in random things or think strangers are signaling to me. That’s not really it.\n\nThis is about work. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. And every time I bring it up, management shows me logs and says, “It’s just policy,” like that settles it.\n\nBut I’ve worked around systems long enough to know logs depend on what people choose to track and how they choose to present it. Sorry, I know I keep coming back to that, but that’s the part people don’t seem to hear. It’s not everything having some hidden meaning. It’s this specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs or ideas that other people just can’t seem to understand, even when you’ve tried to explain them clearly?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s basically why I’m here, I guess.\n\nI’ve tried to explain it at work, and people either don’t understand or they act like I’m making connections that aren’t there. But for over a year now, I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me.\n\nAnd, you know, I worked in computer support long enough to know how these systems look from the inside. It reminds me of when we changed a helpdesk system years ago and suddenly the numbers made one whole team look worse, just because of how categories were assigned. People forget that. They look at a report and think it’s neutral.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying it’s “just policy,” but that’s exactly the sort of thing that can make it harder to explain. Because on paper it looks routine. But I don’t think it is. So yes, I have an idea that other people don’t seem to understand, even when I lay it out pretty clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had experiences where you strongly believed something was true, even when others could not see it or thought you were mistaken, outside of this situation at work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I don’t think so, not in any big way.\n\nI mean, everybody has things they’re sure about when other people don’t agree. Like my brother and I argued for years about whether my father should’ve kept old receipts for tax records—he used to have boxes of them in the basement, stacked by year, and it drove my mother crazy. So I know people can disagree and each person thinks they’re right.\n\nBut if you mean something like this, where I keep trying to explain it and people say I’m mistaken… no, I can’t really think of another example outside work. This is the one that’s been hard to get across.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have beliefs or ideas that you know other people think are strange or impossible, even when you can’t see things any other way?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I know people think it sounds strange. “Impossible,” maybe, depending on who you ask. Management definitely acts like it’s impossible.\n\nBut I can’t really see it any other way. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits aren’t just routine. They’re being used to build a paper trail against me at work. And every time I say that, they pull out logs and say, “See, it’s just policy,” but that doesn’t convince me.\n\nIt’s like—sorry, this reminds me of when we used to troubleshoot printer queues, where the error message wasn’t the real problem, it was just what the system decided to show you. People would stare at the print log like it was the whole truth, but if you knew where to look, you could tell something else was happening underneath.\n\nSo yes, I know other people think I’m reading too much into it. But from where I’m sitting, it fits too well to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe something is happening or being done to you that other people would say is impossible, even when you can’t prove it or show it to them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s what I’m talking about.\n\nI wouldn’t say “impossible,” exactly, because these systems can be used that way. People act like because there’s a log, or because the audit has a policy name attached to it, that proves nobody is steering it. But for over a year now, I’ve felt—more than felt, really—that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.\n\nAnd I know I can’t hand you one document that says, “Here, this is the plan.” That’s the problem. It’s like when an old server starts failing intermittently, and everyone says it passed diagnostics, but you know from the pattern that something’s wrong. I used to see that all the time in support, where the official report missed the real issue.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t settle it for me. To me, the way it lines up is too specific.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something was happening to you that most people would say could not possibly be true, even if you couldn’t prove it to them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s… that’s basically why I’m here. It’s not one single thing I can print out and put on your desk—if it were, this would be a lot easier. It’s the way it keeps lining up.\n\nAnd I’ve worked with systems long enough to know the official screen doesn’t always tell you the real story. You know, back when we had those old help desk dashboards, sometimes the queue looked balanced, but if you actually tracked who got what tickets over a month, you’d see patterns. Anyway—yes. People say it couldn’t be true, but I can’t see it any other way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Delusional Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever have beliefs or ideas that you know other people think are strange or impossible, even when you can’t see things any other way?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I know people think it sounds strange. “Impossible,” maybe, depending on who you ask. Management definitely acts like it’s impossible.\n\nBut I can’t really see it any other way. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits aren’t just routine. They’re being used to build a paper trail against me at work. And every time I say that, they pull out logs and say, “See, it’s just policy,” but that doesn’t convince me.\n\nIt’s like—sorry, this reminds me of when we used to troubleshoot printer queues, where the error message wasn’t the real problem, it was just what the system decided to show you. People would stare at the print log like it was the whole truth, but if you knew where to look, you could tell something else was happening underneath.\n\nSo yes, I know other people think I’m reading too much into it. But from where I’m sitting, it fits too well to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe something is happening or being done to you that other people would say is impossible, even when you can’t prove it or show it to them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s what I’m talking about.\n\nI wouldn’t say “impossible,” exactly, because these systems can be used that way. People act like because there’s a log, or because the audit has a policy name attached to it, that proves nobody is steering it. But for over a year now, I’ve felt—more than felt, really—that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.\n\nAnd I know I can’t hand you one document that says, “Here, this is the plan.” That’s the problem. It’s like when an old server starts failing intermittently, and everyone says it passed diagnostics, but you know from the pattern that something’s wrong. I used to see that all the time in support, where the official report missed the real issue.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t settle it for me. To me, the way it lines up is too specific.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something was happening to you that most people would say could not possibly be true, even if you couldn’t prove it to them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s… that’s basically why I’m here. I know most people hear it and think, “No, that couldn’t be happening,” because management keeps showing me logs and saying the ticket rotation is just policy, the security audits are routine.\n\nBut for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to understand that those things are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. It’s not one single thing I can print out and put on your desk—if it were, this would be a lot easier. It’s the way it keeps lining up.\n\nAnd I’ve worked with systems long enough to know the official screen doesn’t always tell you the real story. You know, back when we had those old help desk dashboards, sometimes the queue looked balanced, but if you actually tracked who got what tickets over a month, you’d see patterns. Anyway—yes. People say it couldn’t be true, but I can’t see it any other way.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever believed something strongly that you knew most people would say is completely untrue, even when you couldn’t show it to them or get anyone else to agree with you—outside of the situation you’ve been describing at work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 60 / computer_support_specialist / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Outside of work? I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in that same way.

I mean, there are things I feel strongly about, sure. Everybody does. Like I’ve always thought people trust automated systems too much—GPS, billing systems, those online forms that reject you because one field is formatted wrong. I could go on about that because I spent years dealing with people who swore the computer was right when it clearly wasn’t, and then you’d find out there was some bad configuration sitting there for six months.

But if you mean something where everyone else says, “That’s completely untrue,” and I’m the only one who sees it… no, I can’t really think of anything outside the work situation. That’s the thing that’s been bothering me.

Management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t mean it’s neutral.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people were trying to harm you or that events were directed at you in situations outside of work, such as in your personal life or other settings?\n\nPatient: In my personal life? No, not really. I mean, I’ve had arguments with people like anyone else, neighbors being noisy, that kind of thing, but I don’t think they’re trying to harm me.\n\nThe thing I keep coming back to is work. That’s where it’s been happening. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to see that the ticket rotation changed, and then the security audits got more pointed, and it’s like they’re building a paper trail on me. Management keeps pulling out logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but I know how those systems work—what gets logged, what gets highlighted, when it gets brought up. That’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the way the ticket rotation and security audits are being handled at work is part of a deliberate, organized effort against you, even if others don’t see it the same way?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, “organized” makes it sound like some movie thing, like people in a dark room with charts on the wall, and I’m not saying that. But deliberate? Yes, I think so.\n\nThe ticket rotation changed, then the security audits started landing in this very particular way, and over time it looks like they’re building a paper trail against me. I’ve worked in computer support long enough to know logs don’t just speak for themselves. Somebody decides what gets tracked, what gets pulled up, what gets treated like a problem.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” but that’s exactly the point. Policy can be used a certain way. And maybe other people don’t see it because they’re not the one it’s aimed at, or they don’t know the systems like I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people involved in changing the ticket rotation and conducting the security audits at work are acting on a shared, secret plan to target you specifically?\n\nPatient: Secret plan… I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly. That sounds a little dramatic. But I do think the ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build something against me, yes.\n\nI mean, I’ve been in computer support a long time, and systems like that don’t just magically decide what matters. Somebody sets them up, somebody decides which logs to show, which incidents get highlighted. Management keeps saying, “It’s just policy,” and showing me reports, but that doesn’t reassure me. Policies can be used to make a paper trail look objective.\n\nSo, shared plan? Maybe not like they all sat down and wrote it out. But I do believe people at work are targeting me specifically through those changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that events, objects, or people’s actions in general — even unrelated to work — have a special, personal meaning for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Not really in general, no. I don’t walk down the street thinking signs or strangers are about me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s work. That’s the part I keep trying to get across. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. And management keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but I know enough about those systems to know policy can be applied in a certain way. So it’s not like everything has a special meaning to me. It’s this specific thing at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that events, objects, or people’s actions in general — even unrelated to work — have a special, personal meaning for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: No, not in the general sense. I mean, I know what you’re asking—like whether I see messages in random things or think strangers are signaling to me. That’s not really it.\n\nThis is about work. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. And every time I bring it up, management shows me logs and says, “It’s just policy,” like that settles it.\n\nBut I’ve worked around systems long enough to know logs depend on what people choose to track and how they choose to present it. Sorry, I know I keep coming back to that, but that’s the part people don’t seem to hear. It’s not everything having some hidden meaning. It’s this specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs or ideas that other people just can’t seem to understand, even when you’ve tried to explain them clearly?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s basically why I’m here, I guess.\n\nI’ve tried to explain it at work, and people either don’t understand or they act like I’m making connections that aren’t there. But for over a year now, I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me.\n\nAnd, you know, I worked in computer support long enough to know how these systems look from the inside. It reminds me of when we changed a helpdesk system years ago and suddenly the numbers made one whole team look worse, just because of how categories were assigned. People forget that. They look at a report and think it’s neutral.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying it’s “just policy,” but that’s exactly the sort of thing that can make it harder to explain. Because on paper it looks routine. But I don’t think it is. So yes, I have an idea that other people don’t seem to understand, even when I lay it out pretty clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had experiences where you strongly believed something was true, even when others could not see it or thought you were mistaken, outside of this situation at work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I don’t think so, not in any big way.\n\nI mean, everybody has things they’re sure about when other people don’t agree. Like my brother and I argued for years about whether my father should’ve kept old receipts for tax records—he used to have boxes of them in the basement, stacked by year, and it drove my mother crazy. This is the one that’s been hard to get across.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have beliefs or ideas that you know other people think are strange or impossible, even when you can’t see things any other way?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I know people think it sounds strange. “Impossible,” maybe, depending on who you ask. People would stare at the print log like it was the whole truth, but if you knew where to look, you could tell something else was happening underneath.\n\nSo yes, I know other people think I’m reading too much into it. But from where I’m sitting, it fits too well to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe something is happening or being done to you that other people would say is impossible, even when you can’t prove it or show it to them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s what I’m talking about.\n\nI wouldn’t say “impossible,” exactly, because these systems can be used that way. People act like because there’s a log, or because the audit has a policy name attached to it, that proves nobody is steering it. But for over a year now, I’ve felt—more than felt, really—that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.\n\nAnd I know I can’t hand you one document that says, “Here, this is the plan.” That’s the problem. To me, the way it lines up is too specific.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something was happening to you that most people would say could not possibly be true, even if you couldn’t prove it to them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s… that’s basically why I’m here. But I do think the ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build something against me, yes.\n\nI mean, I’ve been in computer support a long time, and systems like that don’t just magically decide what matters. Somebody sets them up, somebody decides which logs to show, which incidents get highlighted. Management keeps saying, “It’s just policy,” and showing me reports, but that doesn’t reassure me. Policies can be used to make a paper trail look objective.\n\nSo, shared plan? Maybe not like they all sat down and wrote it out. But I do believe people at work are targeting me specifically through those changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that events, objects, or people’s actions in general — even unrelated to work — have a special, personal meaning for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Not really in general, no. I don’t walk down the street thinking signs or strangers are about me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s work. That’s the part I keep trying to get across. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. And management keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but I know enough about those systems to know policy can be applied in a certain way. So it’s not like everything has a special meaning to me. It’s this specific thing at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that events, objects, or people’s actions in general — even unrelated to work — have a special, personal meaning for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: No, not in the general sense. I mean, I know what you’re asking—like whether I see messages in random things or think strangers are signaling to me. That’s not really it.\n\nThis is about work. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. And every time I bring it up, management shows me logs and says, “It’s just policy,” like that settles it.\n\nBut I’ve worked around systems long enough to know logs depend on what people choose to track and how they choose to present it. Sorry, I know I keep coming back to that, but that’s the part people don’t seem to hear. It’s not everything having some hidden meaning. It’s this specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs or ideas that other people just can’t seem to understand, even when you’ve tried to explain them clearly?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s basically why I’m here, I guess.\n\nI’ve tried to explain it at work, and people either don’t understand or they act like I’m making connections that aren’t there. But for over a year now, I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me.\n\nAnd, you know, I worked in computer support long enough to know how these systems look from the inside. It reminds me of when we changed a helpdesk system years ago and suddenly the numbers made one whole team look worse, just because of how categories were assigned. People forget that. They look at a report and think it’s neutral.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying it’s “just policy,” but that’s exactly the sort of thing that can make it harder to explain. Because on paper it looks routine. But I don’t think it is. So yes, I have an idea that other people don’t seem to understand, even when I lay it out pretty clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had experiences where you strongly believed something was true, even when others could not see it or thought you were mistaken, outside of this situation at work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I don’t think so, not in any big way.\n\nI mean, everybody has things they’re sure about when other people don’t agree. Like my brother and I argued for years about whether my father should’ve kept old receipts for tax records—he used to have boxes of them in the basement, stacked by year, and it drove my mother crazy. So I know people can disagree and each person thinks they’re right.\n\nBut if you mean something like this, where I keep trying to explain it and people say I’m mistaken… no, I can’t really think of another example outside work. This is the one that’s been hard to get across.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have beliefs or ideas that you know other people think are strange or impossible, even when you can’t see things any other way?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I know people think it sounds strange. “Impossible,” maybe, depending on who you ask. Management definitely acts like it’s impossible.\n\nBut I can’t really see it any other way. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits aren’t just routine. They’re being used to build a paper trail against me at work. And every time I say that, they pull out logs and say, “See, it’s just policy,” but that doesn’t convince me.\n\nIt’s like—sorry, this reminds me of when we used to troubleshoot printer queues, where the error message wasn’t the real problem, it was just what the system decided to show you. People would stare at the print log like it was the whole truth, but if you knew where to look, you could tell something else was happening underneath.\n\nSo yes, I know other people think I’m reading too much into it. But from where I’m sitting, it fits too well to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe something is happening or being done to you that other people would say is impossible, even when you can’t prove it or show it to them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s what I’m talking about.\n\nI wouldn’t say “impossible,” exactly, because these systems can be used that way. People act like because there’s a log, or because the audit has a policy name attached to it, that proves nobody is steering it. It’s the way it keeps lining up.\n\nAnd I’ve worked with systems long enough to know the official screen doesn’t always tell you the real story. You know, back when we had those old help desk dashboards, sometimes the queue looked balanced, but if you actually tracked who got what tickets over a month, you’d see patterns. Anyway—yes. People say it couldn’t be true, but I can’t see it any other way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that you knew most people would say is completely untrue, even when you couldn’t show it to them or get anyone else to agree with you—outside of the situation you’ve been describing at work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? No, not really outside of that. I mean, the work thing is the one I keep coming back to, because it’s been over a year of this ticket rotation and the security audits lining up in a way that feels like they’re making a file on me.
